- CAST Freetour 2.0 Pivot 15 -- $829; total 1428 g (115 mm brakes), on-ski skinning 1016 g. CAST launched true hybrid category; skis like regular Look Pivot (15 or 18); swappable toe pieces (pin toe for uphill), brake lock, two heel risers; separate toe pieces to manage; competitive uphill on-ski weight; quick-release toe with little icing risk. Same downhill as Pivot; probably best hybrid for long-term durability; many reviewers' pick for daily resort use. More touring-biased: Shift2 13, Duke PT 11.
- Marker Duke PT 16 (also 11 & 13) -- $749; 1383 g (125 mm brakes), 1074 g skinning. Burliest Marker hybrid; flip alpine toe forward and lock, or remove; transition more involved than Shift2, a tad more finicky than Freetour when removing toe; good uphill/downhill experience. Duke PT 11 much lighter (~850 g uphill); prefer PT 13/16 for frequent resort use. Lighter rugged: Tyrolia Attack Hybrid 14; dedicated touring: Marker Kingpin.
- Tyrolia Attack Hybrid 14 MN/PT -- $599; 1256 g (110 mm brakes), 883 g skinning. Hybrid of Attack alpine and Almonte touring; swap toe pieces on quick-release plate like Attack Demo (thousands of days logged -- optimistic on reliability); a bit more prone to icing (pole whacks fix); lightest hybrid on skin here; "emergency mode" to cautiously ski down in uphill toes as last resort. Reliable for resort laps + occasional skin.
- Salomon/Atomic/Armada Shift2 13 & 16 (16 new) -- $679 (13), $799 (16); Shift2 13: 926 g (120 mm brakes); Shift2 16: 1217 g (110 mm brakes). Convertible toe (pin <-> alpine, no extra pieces). Unlike some first-gen Shifts: brakes stayed up while skinning, no pre-releases, AFD stayed put, taller more functional single riser; toe lever occasionally damaged (rare). Shift2 16: burlier heel like STH2/Strive 16; lean 16 for mostly resort, 13 (and Shift2 10) for regular skintrack use.
- Daymaker Touring Classic Adapters & Tekdapters -- $319 (Classic, 738 g/adapter), $299 (Tekdapters, 540 g). Skin uphill in your regular alpine bindings/skis without drilling; cheaper than touring bindings; heavy, worse on firm sidehill skintracks. Classic works with nearly any boot/binding; Tekdapters need tech-toe boots, lighter, near-frictionless ROM, more compact/torsionally rigid. Easiest, most affordable way to start skinning.
- ATK Hy 13 Free -- $949; 710 g (120 mm brakes). Much lighter than other hybrids, remarkably composed downhill -- clear improvement over pin bindings in variable/chop; impressive touring, transitions more involved; high toe position in "flat" mode helps boots with limited rearward articulation; not yet regular-resort binding (low weight, newness, no alpine release certification). Best downhill of anything equally nice to skin with.
- Marker Kingpin 13 -- $769; 775 g (75-100 mm brakes). Dedicated touring binding for power transfer/control on descent; alpine-style heel more solid than any pin heel; not lightest, not most elastic; prefer DIN-certified hybrids for resort; easier to skin/transition than complex hybrids. More downhill: ATK Hy; lighter similar: Kingpin M-Werks.
- Fritschi Tecton 13 -- $699; stated 630 g (100 mm brakes). Pin toe + alpine-like heel; lateral toe elasticity and vertical heel elasticity -> less jarring, more powerful than lighter bindings; not ATK Hy or hybrid level (not for regular resort); vs Kingpin similar power transfer, a bit lighter, adjustable toe release. Lighter: Vipec, ATK Raider. For those who don't trust full-pin bindings when skiing aggressively.
- G3 ION 12 -- ~$620; 639 g (105 mm brakes). Downhill/feature-biased pin binding; transfers power better than minimal pins, but heavier options offer more elasticity/power; intuitive, reliable, easy risers/touring mode, Pozidriv-serviceable, easy to clear ice. Lighter similar: G3 ZED 12.
- PLUM Karibou 14 Stopper -- EUR599; 595 g (85-100 mm brakes). PLUM's most downhill-oriented; nearly all-metal, rugged; excellent risers; reliable optional brake; respectable power transfer; "elastic response system" spring under toe gives 8 mm elasticity for ski flex; reliable retention/release; brakeless version stated 455 g compelling. With brakes, ATK Raider similar at lower weight.
- Fritschi Vipec Evo 12 -- $599; stated 580 g (100 mm brakes). Same laterally releasable toe as Tecton but pin heel -> simpler, efficient uphill; skis very well for pin binding; very intuitive; easy risers; ski/walk transition without stepping out of toe; smoother ride than most. More downhill: Tecton; lighter easy-use: Marker Cruise, Dynafit Ridge.
- Marker Cruise 12 -- $699; 482 g (105 mm brakes). Built to maximize ease of use; full-featured pin binding (simple brake, forward-pressure compensation, multiple risers); risers easy, stay put; heel rotation effort less than most (except Dynafit Ridge), without removing ski; good suspension for weight; +10 mm heel/toe delta slightly "tip-toey." Balanced and easy to recommend.
- Marker Alpinist Free 13 -- $689; 463 g (105 mm brakes). Alpinist 12 with slightly higher release value and wider composite around toe/heel for wider skis (same mounting pattern); couldn't notice difference vs Alpinist 12 back-to-back. Good suspension, low delta, respectable power for weight; single riser fine for moderate skintracks but can flip inadvertently. Ditch brakes (~100 g) or use brakeless Alpinist 12 (270 g).
- Dynafit Ridge -- $599; 439 g (120 mm brakes). Lightweight with many features: forward-pressure compensation, integrated brakes, easy step-in; "Easy-Turn-Tech" heel dramatically reduces heel-rotation wrestling; no icing/durability issues; very intuitive risers; good descent for ~400 g, nice suspension, neutral +4 mm delta. Well-rounded.
- Salomon MTN Summit 12 -- $629; 410 g (110 mm brakes). Lighter touring alternative to Shift2; builds on MTN Pure with more downhill/features: same excellent risers, easy step-in, elastic forward pressure, adjustable lateral release; less vague power, fewer vibrations; not ATK Raider level but comparable to Alpinist Free 13/Ridge; brakeless ~300 g; brakes among most effective in class (almost same as Shift2).
- G3 ZED 12 -- ~$580; 358 g (no brakes, no leashes). Remarkably similar to heavier ION; same risers; harsher, less power than heavy bindings like Tecton; features comparable to ATK Raider 12 (Raider more solid downhill, ZED similarly nice uphill); elastic heel forward pressure, generous BSL adjustment, multiple risers, adjustable vertical & lateral release. Easier heel rotation: Cruise, Ridge.
- ATK Raider 12 AP -- $659; 352 g (97 mm brakes). Raider platform stands out in 300-400 g class; not as smooth/powerful as Tecton/Kingpin but Blister hasn't used a lighter binding that skis notably better; fantastic risers, excellent build. EVO variants add adjustable toe release & 1-handed brake; Freeraider adds heel spacers for power transfer; all <400 g. Primary reference point for general touring (Hy much better downhill).
- Tyrolia Almonte 12 PT -- $479; 327 g (105 mm brakes). Tyrolia's first pin binding, quickly competitive; ~330 g with brakes; adjustable lateral heel release, fixed forward release via U-spring; gapless heel fit + optional spacer = slightly above-average power transfer; impressively functional mostly internal brake (~65 g savings without); two easy risers; one of easier toe step-ins. Raider/Ridge slightly smoother. Great value.
- Dynafit Blacklight Carbon Pro -- $549; 235 g (no brakes, no leashes). <250 g yet elastic heel travel for ski flex; well-chosen easy climbing aids; predictable retention, confidence-inspiring for weight; best brakeless; with brakes, Ridge/Raider make more sense. For experienced brakeless BC skiers.
- Dynafit Superlite 150 -- $549; 176 g (no brakes, no leashes). Surprising downhill: fairly fast big turns in forgiving snow, half the weight of bindings with similar release values (5-13); 200-lb reviewer in stiff boots drove big skis in most BC conditions; harsher on firm; low ~6 mm delta. Adding brakes/BSL plates makes it closer to "300" and still lacks features. For minimalists.

CLIMBING SKINS (MSRP):
- Big Sky Mountain Products (BSMP) Endurance -- $169-229. BSMP makes skins for many brands + re-glue services; mohair/nylon mixed plush with impressive glide and adequate grip on steep/icy; Free Pro glides a bit better and packs smaller; effective durable hardware for many tip/tail shapes; consistent adhesive with glueless strips -> reliable retention yet easy to rip skis-on. Easy recommendation except max packability.
- BSMP Rover -- $139-199. Full nylon for grip, durability, value; heavier/bulkier; glides as well as any nylon; more traction on firm than many mixed/mohair; very durable after seasons of abuse; hardware like Pomoca's; BSMP can refresh adhesive. Hard to beat for reliability/affordability.
- Black Diamond Ascension -- ~$199. Long a reference for rugged grippy skins; nylon plush similar to before; current adhesive easier to rip without notable adhesion loss; new hardware liked so far; doesn't glide efficiently; grips well, durable, cheaper than mixed. Odd tip/tail shapes: G3 Alpinist Universal.
- Contour Hybrid Mix -- ~$270. Go-to for easiest ripping at transitions; mohair/nylon; Hybrid adhesive far easier to rip than even newer G3/BD glues, still secure; loses stickiness over time but revivable at home; hardware not as secure as G3 on unusual shapes; Free Pro thinner/more packable, Hybrid Mix slightly better grip; durable over many seasons. More efficient: Hybrid Pure.
- Contour Hybrid Pure -- ~$270. All-mohair; glide over grip; same easy adhesive (revivable); not as efficient as Pomoca Race Pro/Free Pro but slightly better traction; less packable; tail clips less secure on twin tails. Tackier hot-melt option: Contour "Guide" series.
- G3 Alpinist Glide -- ~$220. G3 overhauled lineup: new "Channel Bond" adhesive much easier to rip than old tacky glue (early testing), comparable to Pomoca/BSMP; mohair/nylon mix, good grip, slides much better than nylon; impressive hardware for difficult shapes but far more packable than old Alpinist+. Easy recommendation, esp. twin-tip touring setups.
- G3 Alpinist Grip -- ~$200. Full nylon focused on grip & durability; tons of traction on steep firm tracks at cost of glide; among least packable; for those struggling with slippage, esp. newer skiers. Lighter similar grip: Alpinist Universal, BSMP Rover.
- G3 Alpinist Universal -- ~$200. Versatile full nylon; plush like BD Ascension/BSMP Rover; adhesive potentially easiest to rip of the three; hardware versatile and less bulky than before. Mixed plush (Alpinist Glide) better for most; arguably G3's best nylon skin yet.
- Kohla Active Peak -- ~$220. Well-rounded mixed plush with higher-end features at moderate price; thin/light between Pomoca Tour Explore and Free Pro but stiffer -> less packable, durable, less snow creep at ends; mid-pack grip/glide; above-average water resistance; Pomoca-like hardware; "Smart Glue" middle ground. Tackier/cheaper: Kohla Active Basic.
- Montana Montamix -- ~$220. Mixed plush with traditional hot-melt adhesive; like Kohla/Contour Mix plush; waterproof layer for spring; not as packable as Free Pro; clip tip attachment less ideal for narrow/pointy tips; sticks very well but harder to remove -- not for skis-on transitions. Less tacky: Montana "Fusiontec."
- Montana MontaNyl -- ~$200. Full nylon for grip & durability; hot-melt slightly tackier than BD's; solid all-round nylon plush, repels moisture, durable; hardware fine except very pointy/rounded tips. Montamix glides much better, grips marginally worse.
- Pomoca Free Pro -- ~$270. Many reviewers' all-round skin of choice; designed for wide freeride skis -> very low-profile, race-skin-like; packs smaller than most; glides particularly well; respectable grip; wide widths; hardware fits nearly all skis; easy-rip adhesive stickier than Contour Hybrid. Not cheap; thin construction less durable vs rocks/trees.
- Pomoca Tour Explore -- $219-249. Do-everything versatility; glides and packs much better than nylon without much cost or grip loss; easier to rip; one of most versatile. Icy/budget: BSMP Rover; better glide: Free Pro; cheaper similar: BSMP Endurance.
- Pomoca Tour Pro Expedition (newer; early testing) -- ~$270; 120 mm width only. Mixed plush with different fiber executions at center vs edges -> more edge grip without center drag; slightly less glide and slightly larger pack than Free Pro; durable on abrasive spring surfaces; improved tip/tail attachments. Impressive glide with better edge grip than Free Pro.
- Pomoca Race Pro -- ~$190; mostly narrower/shorter sizes. Paul Forward's all-time favorite skin (decades, dozens of skins); most efficient glide here, excellent packability, adhesive holds well yet peels easily; not durable, struggles on steepest/iciest; some reviewers use on much wider skis. Free Pro for race-adjacent performance on wide skis.

BLISTER SPECTRUMS -- Climbing Skins (top -> bottom):
- Best Glide -> Worst: Race Pro & Free Pro (similar); Contour Hybrid Pure, Tour Pro Expedition, BSMP Endurance, G3 Alpinist Glide, Montamix, Contour Hybrid Mix, Tour Explore, Kohla Active Peak (these 8 similar); BSMP Rover, BD Ascension, MontaNyl, G3 Alpinist Universal (these 4 similar); G3 Alpinist Grip.
- Most Grip -> Least: G3 Alpinist Grip; BD Ascension, MontaNyl, BSMP Rover, G3 Alpinist Universal (4 similar); Tour Pro Expedition, Tour Explore, BSMP Endurance, G3 Alpinist Glide, Montamix, Kohla Active Peak, Contour Hybrid Mix (7 similar); Free Pro, Contour Hybrid Pure, Race Pro (3 similar).
- Most Packable -> Least: Race Pro & Free Pro (similar); Tour Pro Expedition, G3 Alpinist Glide, BSMP Endurance, Montamix, Contour Hybrid Mix, Contour Hybrid Pure, Kohla Active Peak, Tour Explore (8 similar); BSMP Rover, G3 Alpinist Universal, BD Ascension, MontaNyl, G3 Alpinist Grip (5 similar).

### Snowboarding & Splitboarding [L13322-]
Reviewers pictured: Justin Bobb (Snowbird; Palisades Tahoe). Section overview: collection of reviewers' favorite snowboards, splitboards, bindings, and splitboard skins with extensive time on most. Specs: stated dims (tip-waist-tail), stated sidecut, profile, lengths, base, core.

SNOWBOARDS:
- Rossignol Juggernaut -- 297-253-297 mm (158); 6.7/7.3 m; 5% tip rocker / 90% camber / 5% tail rocker; 149-162 (incl. 158w, 161w); sintered 7500 base; paulownia/poplar + carbon, kevlar & fiberglass. Freestyle true twin with very burly build: high-speed stability, edge hold, exceptional pop; centered stance for stiff precise park/big features on firm; bindings back for aggressive all-mountain. Like Whitespace Shaun White Pro but softer nose/tail make it more practical off piste. Not accessible/maneuverable in deep variable snow.
- Jones Stratos -- 306-254-296 (159); 7.3 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 153-164W; sintered 9900; poplar/paulownia + basalt stringers & biax fiberglass. Quick-turning directional board for short-radius carves and low-moderate-speed maneuverability, surfy in pow; tight sidecut, accessible flex = minimal input. Vs camber-dominant Nitro Squash: Squash more stable at speed; Stratos quicker, less punishing. Less float than Weston Japow but more practical, better firm grip. Responsive, accessible shorter-turning all-mountain board.
- Cardiff Crane Enduro -- 303-255-295 (158); 7.8 m; rocker/camber/rocker (symmetric rocker, camber between bindings); 146-162; sintered graphite; cedar/aspen + carbon stringers + triax fiberglass. Versatile, freestyle-leaning; moderate flex, quick to pivot in tight terrain, respectable stability; not max-speed firm composure (Cardiff Hawk); predictable in variable without constant input. As versatile as Capita BSOD but damper, smoother in chop. For side-hit seekers.
- K2 Manifest -- 301-255-301 (159); 9.2/7.1/9.2 m; rocker/camber/rocker (subtly directional); 147-164W; sintered 4000; aspen/bamboo/paulownia + carbon stringers + fiberglass. Do-everything freeride/freestyle leaning freestyle; less demanding than prior K2 Antidote; directional twin; decent precision/composure; light lively core -- energetic edge-to-edge. Mid-flex value pick. More stability: Capita Aeronaut; less freestyle: Cardiff Crane.
- Capita Aeronaut -- 301-256-295 (159); 8.0 m; camber with flat zones at tip & tail; 153-161 (incl. wides); sintered HyperDrive ADV XT; poplar/paulownia/bamboo + carbonflax stringers + fiberglass. Fully cambered, mid-stiff, directional board for side hits; slightly tapered but rides like directional twin, intuitive switch; flex makes radius feel shorter -- nimble in bumps/trees/rocks; slightly softer than BSOD, less damp than Crane; accessible pop yet supportive; more composed in choppy runouts than K2 Manifest.
- Cardiff Hawk Enduro -- 304-256-298 (158); 8.0 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 158, 162; sintered graphite; cedar/aspen + carbon stringers + triax fiberglass. Middle between Crane (accessible) and Goat (big-mountain charger), leaning charger; profile like Crane but much stiffer; locked-in, subtle nose rocker -> tracks smoothly through chop; big stability upgrade vs Crane at cost of effort; higher speed limit, precision in steep firm terrain. For aggressive riders at open mountains.
- Whitespace Freestyle Shaun White Pro -- 296-256-296 (158); 8 m; camber; 146-162W; sintered UHMWPE; poplar/paulownia + carbon & fiberglass. Most at home in park on big features/firm snow; true twin, full camber, burly tip-to-tail flex; balanced in air, very supportive landings; stiff nose prone to deflection in variable -- needs attention; low swing weight for stiffness. Stiffer than Juggernaut (which is more predictable outside park). For competition-sized features.
- Nitro Squash -- 305-257-299 (159); 8.4/6.4 m; camber; 156, 159, 163; sintered speed formula HD; poplar/beech + fiberglass. Surf-inspired everyday board; rails powerful turns, commendable edge hold (full camber, progressive sidecut, moderate swallowtail) -- on piste like Jones Hovercraft 2.0; exceptionally poppy; stubby swallow playful yet supportive on jumps (more than Japow/Season Forma); more versatile than most pow swallowtails; nose floats; not chattery on firm. More freestyle: K2 Sky Pilot.
- Burton Family Tree High-Fidelity Camber -- 306-258-296 (158); 7.5 m; rockered nose / camber; 154, 158, 162; sintered WFO; "Super Fly II 700G" core + carbon & fiberglass. Notably damp freeride board for aggressive riders; incredibly locked-in on edge, must drive through turn ends to release; resists low-speed scrubbing -- much more demanding than Crane/BSOD; mutes vibrations in tracked snow. Vs Cardiff Hawk: Hawk a tad more stable; High-Fidelity snappier and easier to turn.
- Weston Dream Machine -- 306-258-306 (161); 7.8/8/7.8 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 143-164w; sintered 4000; poplar/paulownia + carbon stringers + fiberglass. Do-everything standout; directional twin, camber underfoot, medium-stiff -- super playful with spine for soft tracks; less crud-busting than Juggernaut but snappier, less demanding; intuitive edge hold and control. Float: Squash, Sky Pilot. Stiffer: Weston Gnarnia; softer-snow bias: Cardiff Crane.
- Capita Black Snowboard of Death (BSOD) -- 300-259-299 (159); 8.4/8.0/7.8 m; rocker/camber/rocker (mostly camber, early rise nose, flat back); 156-169W; sintered Hyperdrive ADV XT; lightweight-wood/thermopolymer + carbonfleece & carbonflax stringers + fiberglass. Quarter-century-old model, updated; strong freeride board, moderate directional geometry, 20 mm setback; impressive stability/precision, respectable float; freestyle-friendly for its strength; more support than Aeronaut, more accessible than Hawk/High-Fidelity. Easy recommendation for experienced riders.
- K2 Sky Pilot (Sage Kotsenburg signature) -- 308-259-298 (157); 7.5 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 148-163; sintered 4001; bamboo/aspen/paulownia + carbon "Y-beam" + fiberglass. Not pro-only; versatile airtime board; lively core, spooned tip/tail, middle flex -> easy turn initiation, pivots in soft snow; subtle volume shift & 10 mm taper, still intuitive on hardpack and switch; practical 4-6 cm shorter than normal. Not burly. Symmetric alternative: BSOD.
- Lib Tech E-Jack Knife -- 303-259-298 (159); 8.3 m; camber/rocker/camber; 154-168; sintered; aspen/paulownia + fiberglass. Powerful yet freestyle-friendly; standout high-speed stability; camber-dominant, long-feeling sidecut; needs deliberate input, resists low-speed pivots; vs Aeronaut needs more space/speed, sluggish in tight terrain but composed when running. As stiff/precise as Hawk/High-Fidelity, more freestyle-friendly -- big natural features with fast inruns. Firm-snow alternative: Juggernaut.
- United Shapes Horizon -- 302-259-298 (159); 7.8 m; rockered nose / camber; 143-159; sintered 2100; poplar/paulownia/bamboo + carbon stringer + fiberglass. Directional freeride for predictable, composed ride with maneuverability and playfulness; tapered; solid edge hold but jibby; nose float for shallow-moderate pow; softer than Jones Howler/Weston Gnarnia/Head E.B.I. LYT -- easier on firm, cruisy. Playful one-board quiver.
- Season Nexus -- 302-260-296 (158); 7.8 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 143-164; sintered 4001; poplar/paulownia + fiberglass. Season's widest-range board; no single standout trait but makes "blah" resort days fun; lets riders dictate turn shape; easy for newer riders (mellow torsional flex); nice "medium" flex; predictable for side hits/jibbing. Streamlined, adaptable, coherent design.
- Photo: Justin Bobb (Palisades Tahoe).
- TahoeLab Directional -- 303-261-296 (158); 7.8 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 143-181; sintered Durasurf; bamboo + carbon & fiberglass. Reference for composure in unpredictable conditions; built with Freeride World Tour in mind; very stout, durable; best edge grip here; tip/tail rocker more versatile in big mountains than rivals (old Amplid Pentaquark, Ride Commissioner); stiff, needs input, still snappy/playful, minimal chatter. For charging big terrain and non-ideal snow.
- Weston Gnarnia -- 310-262-301 (162); 10/8.1/7.8/9.1 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 143-166W; sintered Durasurf 4001; poplar/paulownia/bamboo + carbon stringers + fiberglass. Stronger, more stable Dream Machine while freestyle-friendly; moderately stiff, impressive snap, lively takeoffs, supportive landings; floats well for class (pow up to ~25 cm, esp. steep); shines in soft chop/mixed; deep carves. More float: Jones Howler.
- Weston Japow -- 317-262-292 (159); 9/6.9 m; rocker/camber/flat; 154-169; sintered Durasurf 4001; bamboo/poplar + carbon tail stringers + fiberglass. Long-time favorite "pow-groomer"; traditional swallowtail, excellent float, encourages wide aggressive turns yet nimble in trees; slower in open terrain than "big gun" Amplid Snommelier but more maneuverable; like a faster, more stable Rossignol XV Sushi; playful, pops off natural hits. One of most well-rounded. Stiffer for chop: Jones Storm Wolf.
- Jones Hovercraft 2.0 -- 311-263-294 (156); 9 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 144-164; sintered 8000 UHMWPE; poplar/paulownia + "Re-Up" stringers (pressed from used snowboards) + flax & fiberglass. Classic surf shape; Re-Up Tech reinforces nose (GEAR:30 podcast ep. 270); large rockered nose floats and busts soft chop; tapered, subtly split tail; mid flex stiffening toward short powerful tail. Aggressive riders may want more at speed/airs; surfy, nimble, accessible pow board, fun on piste. More support: Jones Howler.
- K2 Excavator -- 318-264-298 (154); 6.1/7.1 m; camber; 142-162; sintered 4001; bamboo/aspen/paulownia + carbon stringers + carbon tail "fork" + fiberglass. Volume-shifted surfiness with fewer high-speed compromises; setback stance, stout carbon tail, 20 mm taper; carves hardpack, bounces in soft, plows chop; strong short tail as landing gear; ride shorter (reviewer sized down 7-8 cm) and still stable. Back-foot-dominant style; versatile volume-shift option.
- Jones Storm Wolf -- 303-265-295 (162); 8.8 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 150-162; sintered 8000; paulownia/bamboo + flax & basalt stringers + carbon, innegra & fiberglass. Strong freeride swallowtail; cuts heavy wet pow; snappy on natural features; built for speed, long super-G carves; blends big-gun swallows (Snommelier) and stubby volume-shifted shapes (XV Sushi) -- ample float and speed but agile. For advanced/expert. More slow-speed accessibility: Jones Storm Chaser.
- Never Summer Proto Type 3 -- 312-265-312 (161X); 7.8 m; "Triple Camber Recurve" (camber-dominant feel); many lengths incl. DF/X; sintered Durasurf XT 5501; poplar/paulownia/birch + carbon "matrix" & stringers + fiberglass. Adaptable true twin; stitched carbon at tips/tails for targeted torsional stiffness -- easy to flex in middle, stiff laterally at ends; impressive damping -- stable, chatter-free on firm and in chop for a mid-stiff board; works with slight setback. All-mountain freestyle for aggressive riders not drawn to directional freeride boards.
- Ride Warpig -- 316-265-306 (151); 5.4/6.5 m; rocker/flat/rocker; 142-158; sintered 4000; aspen/bamboo/paulownia + impact binding plates + carbon stringers + fiberglass. Volume-shifted freestyle board, surprisingly adaptable; stable at speed, drives through crud; skate-style feel for ollies/spins; strong low-rise tail more stable than pin-tailed rivals; mid-wide good for big feet; not quickest edge to edge; stiffer park board for bigger jumps.
- Soul Stick Shift Elite -- 325-267-290 (159); 9/6.9 m; camber with 3D contouring; 159, 163; sintered 7700; poplar/paulownia + carbon stringers + fiberglass. Special volume-shifted swallowtail for aggressive riders used to big freeride boards; longer than similar boards (plus at speed); one of dampest boards tested -- nearly impossible to unsettle; set-back stance for back-foot slashes; big 3D nose floats and blows through chop. Not balanced in air or nimble in tight terrain. Versatile alternative: Japow.
- Jones Howler (Victor de Le Rue & Elena Hight input) -- 312-267-304 (160W); 8.6 m; camber; 152-163W; sintered 8000; paulownia/bamboo/poplar + carbon/flax stringers + koroyd tip inserts + fiberglass. For modern big-mountain freestyle; high-speed stability with agility and pop; subtle taper, buoyant nose; stiff, full camber -> edge hold, rebound, landing support; composed across groomers, soft chop, crud; playful supportive tail for switch. E.B.I. LYT and Squash better carvers; otherwise huge versatility.
- Season Forma -- measured 319-267-308 (153); 7 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 144, 153, 159; sintered 4000; poplar/paulownia + fiberglass. Easy first surf-inspired pow board, between short slashers and long arcers; fast in deep snow, float, playful; like Japow but easier to throw around and slower in very deep; decent groomer edge hold. Stiffer: Storm Wolf; max maneuverability: Storm Chaser.
- United Shapes Deep Reach -- 322-272-302 (162); 8.6 m; tip rocker / camber; 149-162; sintered 2100; poplar/paulownia + carbon weave & stringers + fiberglass. Blast in fresh snow but stands out moving into chop, firm groomers; abundant float; supportive tail; less surfy than Storm Chaser but far more planted at speed; excels in tracked-out chop -- stiff/damp yet torsionally maneuverable; predictable on piste (not Japow-level carver). Whole resort pow day board.
- Ride Superpig -- 301-270-291 (154); 5.6/6.9 m; tip rocker / camber; 142-158; sintered 4000; aspen/bamboo/paulownia + carbon stringers + carbon & fiberglass. Warpig concept with more precision/stability; stubby shape floats impressively but less maneuverable in deep snow than Nexus or Deep Reach; sharp snappy carves; more comfortable at speed (needs more speed); freestyle-capable for aggressive riders; trades skate maneuverability for firm grip. More freeride maneuverability: Sky Pilot.
- Head E.B.I. LYT -- 311-272-306 (161); 8.6/7.4 m; rocker/camber/flat/camber/rocker; 143-161; sintered; "lightweight wood" + "Hexagonal Core" tip & tail inserts + graphene + fiberglass. For on-piste enjoyment plus deeper days; balances float/agility with uncompromised edge hold; wide rockered nose and swallowtail float in light/moderate snow; soft nose can fold in dense heavy snow at speed (Howler/Storm Wolf better there). Shines on groomers -- carving joy, accessible flex.
- Jones Storm Chaser -- 324-275-304 (152); 6.9 m; tip rocker / camber; 142-157; sintered 8000; paulownia/poplar + flax & carbon stringers + fiberglass. Size down several cm; lots of volume in short nimble swallowtail; agile dynamic pow-groomer not overly specialized; surprisingly fast in deep, easy to get on step, maneuverable on low angles vs Forma/XV Sushi; less fast/stable than Storm Wolf/Japow in bottomless/chop; remarkable carver (like Squash but easier to slash).
- Rossignol XV Sushi -- 340-276-282 (145W); 8/9.5 m; 30% rocker / 60% camber / 10% rocker; 144, 145W; sintered 7500; poplar + urethane strips + fiberglass. Extreme short-and-stubby: 340 mm nose, ~60 mm taper, 88 mm tail, 145 cm -- "powder surfer you can bolt bindings to"; front-foot-heavy in pow; calm in low-density chop, loses composure in firmer crud at speed (vs Japow/Forma); blast carving groomers (no toe/heel drag). Pair with stiff binding. More versatile: Storm Chaser, Excavator, Squash.
- Photo: Justin Bobb (Snowbird).

SNOWBOARD BINDINGS:
- Nidecker OG Supermatic -- $430; flex 3/5; M, L, XL. Step down on highback to lock and tighten straps; flip rear lever to release; extremely efficient though precision-focused riders may still tighten buckles; not very stiff or light. More support: LT Supermatic, Carbon Supermatic.
- Jones Mercury FASE -- $370; 7/10; M, L. Fast Entry System (used across brands): rides like a normal two-strap binding; highback pivots back for step-through, step down to engage, couple clicks of alloy buckles; no special boots; lively medium-stiff highback, supportive yet tweakable, park to pow. Vs Supermatic: similar efficiency, lighter and lower-profile, more natural feel. Stiffer: new Zenith FASE.
- Arbor Cypress -- $300; medium-stiff; S/M, M/L, L/XL. Well-rounded mid-stiff; plant-based plastic straps wrap foot but give for jibbing; responsive side; like Mercury FASE stiffness; X-shaped baseplate & foam preserve board flex (like NOW/Yes SkateTech); highly adjustable; durable for price. Stiffer without harshness.
- Union Source FC -- $1000; 8/10; M, L. Lightweight, stiff, responsive; "Direct Connect" mounting replaces disc with screws and triangular washers -> mirrors board's natural flex better than most; "Forged Carbon" construction; minimalist straps; slightly harsh on firm/choppy snow vs padded bindings. High-performance for intermediate-expert.
- Jones Zenith FASE (new) -- $490; 10/10; M, L. Stiffer freeride FASE option; carbon construction, precise edging; EVA cushioning keeps it smooth; slightly softer than Jones Apollo Pro, much stiffer than Mercury FASE, near Union Source FC; pairs with stiff/wide boards. Fast-entry convenience in a notably stiff binding.
- Jones Apollo Pro -- $650; 10/10; M, L. Precision and power for carving firm or blowing through crud; NOW/Yes SkateTech chassis (center pivot) reduces fatigue, boosts edging; stiff asymmetrical carbon/flax highback; smooth transitions; ankle straps flip "freeride mode" -> "surf mode" for mobility, swappable softer bushings. For aggressive riders on stiff/wide boards.
- Photo: Justin Bobb (Snowbird).

SPLITBOARDS:
- Burton Family Tree High-Fidelity Camber Split -- 293.5-250-286.5 (158); 7.8 m; rockered nose / camber; 154, 158, 162; sintered WFO; "Super Fly II 700G" + carbon & fiberglass. Stiff charging solid board in split form; camber-dominant = excellent traction on steep firm skintracks; camber through tail = locked-in, needs deliberate release; much harder to pivot in trees than Cardiff Goat (rockered tail). Specialized for aggressive riders wanting uphill grip and open-terrain stability. More versatile: Cardiff Goat, Weston Backwoods Carbon.
- Jones Solution Split -- 311-254-298 (164); 9.3 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 154-169w; sintered 9900; paulownia/poplar + flax & basalt stringers + fiberglass. Excels in any soft snow (corn bowls, pow trees); floats, big arcs or short slashes; speed limit and chatter for experts in firm/variable -- which makes it even better for less experienced riders; easy kick turns; dual-serrated edges grip when split. Ultralight Solution for more support/less weight.
- Prior Khyber Split ("XTC" lightweight build; custom available) -- 314-256-287 (160); 8.0 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 150-170; sintered 4001 Durasurf; yellow-cedar/maple/red-cedar + carbon (custom). Pow-focused without being pow-only; wide nose/narrow tail float, reduce leg fatigue; not as fast in deep as swallowtails (Never Summer Swift) but more playful/balanced; especially poppy in XTC (heavier fiberglass version exists); for pillows, features, trees; a bit lighter than Venture Paragon, similar overall. Surf tool: Weston Japow Split.
- Jones Stratos Split (Ultralight Stratos) -- 305-256-295 (159); 7.5 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 156, 159, 161W; sintered 8000; paulownia/poplar + carbon/flax stringers + fiberglass. Playful easygoing BC versatility; 3D-spooned tip/tail reduce catchiness, still responsive on hard snow; generalist with deep-snow and BC-freestyle bias; livelier than Solution/Hovercraft 2.0 with less float; ~3000 g @159; softer, less steady than Backwoods Carbon. Surfier/lighter: Ultralight Hovercraft.
- Weston Backwoods Carbon Split -- 312-259-294 (160); 7.7/6.9/8 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 152-167W; sintered 4000 electra; paulownia/bamboo + vertical aluminum stringers + carbon. Versatile; floats, grips, composed on serious descents; moderately directional; stout flex edges in variable at speed; 1-split-quiver contender vs Venture Paragon/Prior Khyber -- stiffest of the three yet light (~2900 g @160); reliable uphill; one of most dependable when letting it run; more accessible slow than Cardiff Goat Pro Carbon. Surfier: Ultralight Hovercraft.
- Weston Japow Split -- 317-262-292 (159); 9/6.9 m; rocker/camber/flat; 154-169; sintered Durasurf 4001; bamboo/poplar + carbon tail stringers + fiberglass. Favorite pow board solid or split; 25 mm taper, forward-weighted comfortable stance; tight rear sidecut for quick turns; ~3270 g @159, durable; reviewer in Hokkaido kept swapping back to it; predictable on firm. Weston's recommended skins lacked coverage on steep sketchy ascents; check tail clip fit with metal notches. Lighter: Cardiff Powgoda Pro Carbon.
- Venture Storm Split (Carbon version lighter) -- 314-265-305 (162); 7.92 m; rocker/flat/rocker; 152-167; sintered p-tex; poplar/ash + fiberglass. Adaptable freeride all-rounder for alpine faces, trees, variable; 9 mm taper; floats, turns easily, reliable on skin track (less slippage than some rockered splits); Venture known for burly durable builds, now lighter. Freestyle: Venture Paragon; surfier: Venture Euphoria.
- Cardiff Goat Pro Carbon Split ("Enduro" cheaper/heavier/softer) -- 301-265-290 (162); 11 m avg.; rocker 40% / camber 60%; 150-166; sintered graphite; poplar/paulownia + titanal stringers + carbon. Uncommon combo: grip, low weight (~3085 g @162), tons of pop; durable, predictable in rough snow; slightly tapered/setback for float and stability yet balanced in air; great edge hold; one of liveliest splits despite stiffness. Precision/support for big days with features.
- Venture Paragon Split (carbon version lighter) -- 315-265-307 (159); 7.61 m; rocker/flat/rocker; 149-168; sintered p-tex; poplar/ash + fiberglass. Rarely out of place; modestly directional but freestyle-friendly; especially good in chop/crud; maneuverable in trees, spins/switch; dependable grip on traverses and icy couloirs; not light but very durable; damp for moderate flex; more stable than Jones Stratos/Solution, more freestyle than Backwoods/Powgoda. More directional: Venture Storm.
- Jones Ultralight Hovercraft 2.0 Split -- 316-267-299 (160); 9.3 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 152, 156, 160; sintered 9900; paulownia/poplar + koroyd + carbon & fiberglass. Blast to carve/slash most BC conditions; excellent uphill; large spooned nose keeps tip up, catch-free; ~2700 g @160, no durability compromise noticed; flex matches shape (stable at speed, easy to flick); "Traction Tech" serrations on both sides of each ski for icy skintracks; like discontinued Amplid Millisurf (high praise).
- Cardiff Powgoda Pro Carbon Split -- 314-272-294 (158); 8.4 m; rocker 50% / camber 50% (rocker mostly in nose); 148-162; sintered graphite; poplar/paulownia + titanal stringers + carbon. Nimble, excels in fresh snow without being liability when forecast fails; 2984 g @158; moderately tapered tail, very good float; progressive sidecut: back-foot for trees, front-foot for big turns. Goat Pro Carbon more freestyle/stable on firm; Powgoda much more deep-snow maneuverability.

SPLITBOARD BINDINGS:
- Spark R&D Arc ST Pro -- $669; stated 556 g (M); XS-L. Best for uphill efficiency and surfy downhill; Pro trims weight with premium materials with few compromises; lightest here, performs almost identical to Arc ST, no durability red flags after multiple seasons; carbon-infused asymmetric highback marginally stiffer; Spark pucks; Ibex ST Pro Crampon ~120 g each. Standard Arc ST ~630 g, cheaper.
- Voile Light Speed -- $295; stated 750 g (M); S, M, L. Reliable, value-packed; "Quick Lock" bracket similar to Spark Tesla T1; solid metal pins mounted to board (less chance of falling out); many screws/bolts (repairable but more to monitor); more adjustability; slightly more icing-prone; some prefer its risers/straps; downhill like Spark Surge ST; heavier but much cheaper.
- Spark R&D Surge ST -- $529; stated 689 g (M); XS-L. Stiffer all-round Spark; more power than Arc ST; Tesla T1 system impressive for ease and durability; "Pillow Line" straps light, comfortable, shed snow, more durable than stitched straps; not as stiff as Karakoram Straightline/X Carbon but more versatile and much better value. Lighter: Surge ST Pro.
- Spark R&D Nick Russell Pro -- $819; stated 638 g (M); XS-L. Surge ST Pro baseplate + lighter Arc ST Pro straps + pro hardware; strong lateral support on technical ascents; agile powerful downhill; new "Multi-Hole Pro Heel Loop" = 3 highback rotation positions to align carbon-reinforced highback with heel edge; stripped aluminum hardware, pre-installed EVA pads. Stiff binding power with precise highback alignment.
- Spark R&D Surge ST Pro -- $719; stated 610 g (M); XS-L. Lives up to hype: ~70 g lighter per foot than Surge ST without durability/performance loss; asymmetric "Rip 'N' Flip" highbacks for lateral movement and natural stance; drives big boards without harshness; go-to for several reviewers. More highback adjustability: Nick Russell Pro.
- Nitro Vertical ST -- $530; stated 725 g (M); M, L. Nitro's take on Spark Tesla T1 chassis; responsiveness and comfort -- EVA baseplate foam, padded ankle strap, cable-reinforced ladders/buckles; stiff highback a tad stronger than Surge; quick forward-lean with customizable block; "Whammy Bar" heel riser easy and secure. More resort-like ride for a few extra grams; less long-term data than Spark.

SPLITBOARD CLIMBING SKINS:
- Kohla Smart Skins (also branded by Karakoram & Nitro) -- waterproof "Fiber Seal" backing prevents soaking/icing; less tacky "Smart Glue" easy to rip; adaptive to nearly any split shape/length; hardware more versatile than old BD STS; 65% mohair / 35% nylon -- great grip-to-glide, surprisingly grips a tad better than G3 nylon Universal; caveat: tail clip durability issues. Kohla phasing out Smart Glue; same plush/hardware with tackier hot-melt.
- G3 Splitboard Universal -- new "Channel Bond" adhesive (early testing): much easier to rip, no skin-savers needed for storage, no notable adhesion drop; hardware accommodates many tail shapes, more durable than Kohla's; nylon plush reliable grip, respectable glide like original BD Ascension. Also "Grip" and mohair-mix "Glide" plushes.
- Jones Nomad (made by Pomoca) -- for Jones splits post-2016: pre-cut or trim-to-fit Nomad and Nomad Pro; 70% mohair / 30% nylon versatile like Kohla's; less efficient than G3 Glide on low angles but grips better on steep/icy; Nomad Pro adds Pomoca Glide treatment; trim-to-fit uses standard tail clip, pre-cut needs tail notch (works great on Ultralight Hovercraft 2.0); glue reliable with care; thin, light, packable.

### Outerwear [L14699-]
Reviewers pictured: Luke Koppa (Crested Butte), Sascha Anastas (Snowbird). Section overview: apparel matters (soaked shells, steamy pants); a few favorites from light breathable touring to burly resort pieces; much more on website ("Apparel" category, 25/26 Outerwear Roundup). Women's (first two pages) vs men's (following) organized by tester gender.

WOMEN'S-TESTED (MSRP; measured weight; fabric):
- Flylow Billie Coat & Foxy Gore-Tex Bib -- $480 jacket / $700 bib; 684 g (M) / 690 g (size 8); jacket 3L Surface laminate (150D), bib 3L Gore-Tex (50D). Foxy is a cult classic that popularized women's bibs; new premium Gore-Tex variant; Billie Coat high-end performance without Gore-Tex price. Numerical women's bottom sizing (size 8 ~ Medium). Tons of storage; internal & external leg vents; great storm collar; hood shorter (helmet compatible) can tug collar. Middle-of-road fit -- less freeride than Trew/Stio, not as slim as Peak Performance Alpine.
- Obermeyer Steibis Jacket & Bib -- $629 / $529; 719 g / 628 g (M); 3L HydroBlock. Rugged yet comfortable, supple, reliable in wet snow; "freeride-inspired" longer cut, moderately loose; lots of storage but no external chest pocket; narrow elastic cuffs not easy over thick gloves; underarm vents; bib dropseat zips double as vents. Similar to Trew Stella/Chariot but softer hand and slightly sleeker.
- Peak Performance Alpine Gore-Tex C-Knit Jacket & Pant -- $750 / $650; 550 g / 482 g (S); 3L Gore-Tex C-Knit (70D). Designed 17 years ago for Alps guides, redesigned for resort and BC; C-Knit softer and quieter than standard Gore/Gore Pro; excellent water resistance, at least average breathability; Gore-Tex Stretch panel upper back; sleek, notably less baggy, long sleeves/legs; removable powder skirt, internal hand gaiters, radio antenna slot, goggle wipe, double-zip vents, waist adjusters, helmet hood.
- Stellar Equipment Pro Shell Jacket & Bib -- $750 / $650; 597 g / 718 g (M); 3L Dermizax NX (70Dx140D). Burly Japanese fabric; reliable water resistance, slightly above-average breathability; "regular" fit; marketed for touring but excellent 50/50 kit; big standing collar; one of better, bigger helmet hoods; stowable powder skirt; long torso/sleeves; bib double side zips as vents, stretchy non-waterproof torso fabric for breathability.
- Stio Figment Jacket & Bib -- $529 / $499; 780 g / 822 g (M); 3L PeakProof (150D). For shell longevity, longer roomier fit; high-end protection; super burly 150D face shrugs off branches/ski edges; cheaper than similarly protective shells; long relaxed fit like Trew but not as loose at knees/waist; lots of pockets and venting; not packable. Trew/Obermeyer more supple.
- Trew PRIMO Stella Jacket & Chariot Bib -- $599 / $599; 824 g / 962 g (M); 3L PNW Primo (recycled face). Favorite resort kit, holds up to daily lift laps; nearly as burly as Figment but less stiff/crinkly; most relaxed women's fit here; not light/packable; many vents incl. inner/outer leg vents; ample storage; Stella's big hood among best used, collar stays up comfortably. Slimmer: Flylow, Peak Performance.

MEN'S-TESTED:
- Flylow Gore-Tex Lab Jacket & Compound Pant -- $700 / $575; 659 g / 602 g (M); 3L Gore-Tex (recycled 50D polyester). Top of Flylow line; balance of protection and weight for BC + resort; not as burly as Trew PRIMO but lighter, more breathable; fully featured, regular fit; excellent weather resistance -- pick for waterproofing; but Flylow's in-house 20k/20k laminates (Chemical Pant, Quantum Pro Jacket) are extremely similar and cheaper unless riding in rain.
- Jones Deeper Gore-Tex Jacket & Pant (new) -- $490 / $380; 730 g / 634 g (L); 2L Gore-Tex ePE (recycled 75D + lining). More relaxed, resort-oriented; 2L gets same water/breath ratings as 3L at lower price (heavier); room for puffy layers without excess; plenty of storage; venting for bootpacks; hanging liner feels warmer; insulated version available. Lighter/breathable: Jones "Higher" and "Further."
- Patagonia PowSlayer Jacket & Pant -- $799 / $719; 630 g / 550 g (L); 3L Gore-Tex Pro (ePE, 80D recycled). Maybe best PowSlayer yet; latest Gore-Tex ePE Pro performs like last-gen Gore Pro except PFAS-free DWR beads water for less time (industry-wide reality); go-to for weather resistance; rugged for its light weight -- great 50/50 or wet-climate touring; middle fit; dialed pockets; hood pulls over helmet without undoing main zipper. Cheaper comfy: Patagonia SnowDrifter.
- Peak Performance Vertical Gore-Tex Pro Jacket & Bib -- $850 / $750; 686 g / 635 g (M); 3L Gore-Tex Pro (recycled nylon). Flagship; water resistance/breathability nearly identical to PowSlayer but long, roomy "freeride" fit still sleek; heaps of features, long vents, excellent hood with tall collar; durable for daily resort use after ~2 seasons; adequate for BC. Cheaper: Peak Performance Edge and Vertec.
- Raide TourTech Shell Jacket & Bib -- $599 / $449; 408 g jacket / 670 g bib (M); jacket 3L TourShell-01 (20D); bib 3L TourShell-01 (40Dx70D) + 70D softshell. BC-specific brand's first line; ultralight jacket breathes better than nearly any waterproof shell tested; bib vents even better (non-waterproof softshell over thighs/upper back legs); not for frequent resort use; can let water through in extended rain/sleet; one of all-time favorite touring kits for drier ranges (southern Rockies); two skin-sized pockets on both pieces with electronics sleeves.
- Trew PRIMO Cosmic Jacket & Eagle Pant -- $599 / $499; 800 g / 905 g (M); 3L PNW Primo (recycled face). Go-to daily resort choice; burly but supple; 250+ days in Cosmic without a tear, still dry; Eagle Pant survived well past the 25-50 days in which a reviewer usually slashes pant cuffs; generous pockets; Cosmic wide, less long than before; Eagle fits like Flylow Compound. Bib: Trewth Bib PRIMO. Heavy -- not for touring (Trew has lighter BC lines).

### Helmets & Goggles [L14990-]
Reviewers pictured: Kristin Sinnott, Justin Bobb (Snowbird). Section overview -- Helmets: no one should ski/ride without a properly fitting helmet; founder Jonathan Ellsworth, while bootpacking on a summer ski tour, fell off a cliff, tomahawked through a boulder field, and broke his neck -- helmet saved his life (he and witnesses believe); skiing six months later after major surgery (Blister Podcast episodes #47-48). Goggles: avoiding crashes requires seeing well in all light; lens/lens-swap improvements trickling down to cheaper models; alternatives mentioned beyond pictured items.

HELMETS (MSRP; measured weight):
- Anon Logan WaveCel -- $260; 531 g (L). WaveCel 3D-matrix between shell and head flexes/crumples/glides on impact ("network of hundreds of interconnected shock absorbers"); bold claims re rotational forces; light, low-profile, well-ventilated; BOA fit, Fidlock magnetic buckle. Vs Giro Owen: Owen more airflow moving, Logan's top vents release heat standing still. Also likes Anon Merak WaveCel ($290; adjustable vents, hybrid shell); cheaper WaveCel: Oslo, Windham.
- Giro Owen Spherical -- $280; 521 g (L). Reviewer favorite; high-end protection and features, sleek minimal skate look; dial fit works for varied head shapes; front/rear vents move air while moving; fairly light; Fidlock; Mips Spherical (inner foam shell rotates within outer) -- quieter, more comfortable than thin-liner Mips. Ventilation: Giro Grid Spherical, Tor Spherical; value Mips: Giro Ledge ($125).
- Glade Boundary Mips -- $199; 421 g (M). Lots of features, lightweight; very well ventilated with independently adjustable front/back vents; Glade "MagFit" goggles auto-align via brim magnets (reduces gaper gap); dial adjuster works with mittens; Fidlock; Mips. Compelling value. Sleeker: Glade Highland, Tenmile 2.
- POC Fornix Mips -- skate-inspired look with higher-end features; lightweight...
  ...$230; 490 g (XL/XXL); large adjustable vents (lift or human-powered); functional dial; traditional buckle (not missed); like Giro Owen/Anon Logan -- good ventilation, moderate weight, high-end protection below flagship prices. Different look: POC Obex Mips ($250; lighter, optional pricey "Connect" audio).
- Pret Vision X (women's) / Fury X (men's) -- $230; 473 g (M). All Pret helmets have Mips; mid-priced; adjustable ventilation, multi-shell, Fidlock, antimicrobial foam; low weight; Pret helmets pair with broad range of goggles. Also tested Corona X/Cirque X and Lyric X2/Cynic X2 ($160, great value; heavier, less vented); budget Epic X Mips ($120). Fans of fit, comfort, durability.
- Salomon MTN Lab -- $290; 379 g (M). Multi-norm certified (ski, mountaineering, bike); helped launch category; favorite for ski mountaineering; works with most goggles, looks normal; super light, tons of ventilation; more descent coverage than climbing helmet. Lift-only: heavier helmets or Salomon Husk Prime.
- Smith Summit Mips -- $260; 404 g (M). Ski touring + mountaineering certification; headlamp routing, bungee pack lashing, compact BOA, many vents; thin earpad-less knit liner (bare head, beanie, cap); not ideal for resort (Smith Vantage 2 Mips $295, Method Mips $160); poor fit with some non-Smith goggles with curved frames. Purpose-built BC helmet.
- Sweet Protection Igniter 2Vi Mips -- $280; 721 g (M/L). Rugged build; "2Vi" system = hard shell + shock-absorbing foam + 2-piece Mips liner integrated for weight/comfort; dial adjuster, Fidlock, multiple vents; go-to resort helmet for some reviewers. Burlier: Trooper 2Vi ($350); cheaper/lower-profile: Looper Mips ($180).

GOGGLES (MSRP; included lenses):
- Anon M5 / M5.S -- $320 (2 Perceive lenses + MFI face mask). Anon started the magnetic lens-swap trend ("Magna Tech"); mid-size; "Flat Toric Lenses" combine toric's reduced distortion/FOV with cylindrical look; ridiculously easy yet secure swaps; magnetic MFI face masks pair seamlessly. Bigger: M4; cheaper magnetic: Sync ($210, two Perceive lenses).
- Dragon DX3 Plus OTG -- $160 (2 Lumalenses). Traditional (slower) swap vs Dragon RVX Mag, but two high-contrast Lumalens lenses at low price; "medium" fit; "armored" venting resists snow clogging; choose variant with one bright and one low-light lens; optics like goggles costing twice as much.
- Glade Adapt 3 -- $169 (1 Reveal 4K photochromic lens). Value/versatility via photochromic lenses; Reveal 4K contrast comparable to high-end, VLT ~45% to 7% (CAT 1-4); "one goggle for everything"; "medium-large" fit, small and low-bridge variants; "MagFit" magnets auto-align with Glade helmets. Magnetic swap: Glade Fathom 2.
- Julbo Launcher Reactiv -- $320 (1 Reactiv lens). Photochromic leader + "SuperFlow Pro" ventilation (pop lens out slightly to vent for bootpacks/skinning); tested "Reactiv 0-4 High Contrast" -- very wide range, covers low-light storm days slightly better than most photochromics; not designed for easy lens swaps; "medium" fit. Without vent feature: Julbo Shadow, Cyrius X.
- SHRED Gratify -- $250 (1 CBL 2.0 lens). Popular; proprietary "Contrast Boosting Lens 2.0" (Ice) -- excellent clarity/contrast sunny to fairly gray; single-lens versatility (slow swaps); fairly large frame still fits medium-helmet reviewers; no old cylindrical distortion. GEAR:30 ep. 272 with SHRED founder Ted Ligety.
- Smith Squad Mag -- $265 (2 ChromaPop lenses). Quick Mag swap (hair more involved than Anon, extra latch for security); big cylindrical frame for medium/medium-large faces; also I/O Mag (spherical, multiple sizes) and 4D Mag (more downward FOV). Cheaper: standard Squad and new Stash XL ($165 with two ChromaPop lenses).
- SPY Marauder Elite Eco -- $340 (2 Happy Boost lenses). Happy Boost among best lenses tested (contrast/saturation); flagship toric with quick magnetic swap; medium-large frame, thicker frame/foam can protrude with shallow helmet brims. Other Happy Boost frames: Waypoint (spherical), Megalith & Marshall 2.0 (~$200), standard cylindrical Marauder ($280).
- Sweet Protection Boondock II (new) -- $200 (1 RIG lens). Low-profile, rugged, comfortable, wide FOV; burly 2.8 mm cylindrical lens; S/M/L frames; RIG lenses strong contrast; tested RIG Topaz (VLT 12%) excelled in bright "blinding graybird" and shadows; tight helmet interface. Two lenses/faster swap: Sweet Protection Interstellar ($335).

## 4. Blister 'Best Of' Awards [L15290-15682]
Reviewers pictured: Luke Koppa (Crested Butte), Jonathan Ellsworth (Snowbird). Framing: products that are truly exceptional, not necessarily best for you. Rules: (1) between similar performers, pick the one logged most time on -- don't care if new, care if good; (2) only gear they can vouch for (criticizes industry awards bought via ad deals or given to barely tested gear); (3) no company paid for inclusion or awards; (4) NEW THIS YEAR: awards reflect reviewer consensus rather than individual reviewers' personal favorites -- if others disagreed, it wasn't included.

- ALL-MOUNTAIN SKIS: Black Crows Mirus Cor (makes no sense on paper; all-time favorite for fun on low-angle groomers & features); Folsom Spar 88 (favorite sub-90 mm true all-mountain); Salomon QST 94 & 100 (forgiving, playful, yet skiable hard esp. moguls); Line Blade (exhilarating tight turns, surprisingly adaptable); Line Optic 88, 96, 104 & 114 (suspension + freestyle playfulness); K2 Mindbender 96 (as adaptable as 99Ti, comparable stability, lower weight); Season Kin (tight-turning carver, exceptionally playful); Armada AntiMatter 100 (keeps Declivity X 102 traits, more on-piste precision); Volkl Mantra 102 (damp, stable, accessible for strong-ski fans); Icelantic Nomad 100, 106 & 112 (easygoing + high-speed composure; beginners to experts); Moment Countach 110 (all reviewers got along with it; broadly appealing soft/mixed-snow ski).
- FREESTYLE SKIS: Faction Studio 2; K2 Reckoner 102; Rossignol Sender 110 (freestyle shape + charger construction for modern freestyle big-mountain); Dynastar M-Free 112 (more forgiving/surfy Sender 110, extremely damp; 190 cm a favorite "playful charger" incl. directional reviewers).
- POWDER SKIS: RMU Apostle 114 (light, agile, well-rounded); Prior CBC (first pick for steep pillow lines); Rossignol Sender 118 (managing editor's all-time favorite; chop nearly as fun as untracked); Folsom Rapture (Paul Forward's go-to big-mountain pow ski); Heritage Lab HB122 V2 ("almost magical" forgiving, supportive tail; V2 stiffer, reshaped, more powerful tip -- one of most fun resort pow skis).
- WOMEN'S SKIS: Elan Ripstick 94 W; Blizzard Black Pearl 94; Volkl Mantra 84 W, M7 Mantra W & Mantra 102 W; Armada AntiMatter 100 W; Icelantic Maiden 102 & 108; Wagner Summit 106 (all reviewers liked it; great 50/50 or lighter all-mountain).
- 50/50 SKIS: Atomic Bent 110 & Bent Chetler 120 (extremely playful, light); 4FRNT Hoji (skis well for weight, unusually adaptable).
- TOURING SKIS: Majesty Superwolf (spring touring favorite); 4FRNT Raven (light but hard to unsettle); Heritage Lab BC120 (light, intuitive big-mountain pow).
- TOURING BINDINGS: ATK Raider 12 (benchmark for versatility in 300-400 g class).
- SKI BOOTS: Atomic Hawx Ultra 130; Lange Shadow 130 LV & Shadow 115 W LV; Salomon S/Pro Alpha C BOA 130 (fit versatile for some LV and some MV reviewers); Lange Code 140 (new benchmark for walk-mode boots); Tecnica Cochise 130; Atomic Hawx Ultra XTD 130 & XTD 115 W; Tecnica Zero G Tour Pro & Tour Pro W; Scarpa F1 LT (on its way out, pushed ~1 kg category forward); Tecnica Zero G Peak Carbon; Salomon S/Lab MTN Summit; ZipFit Liners (most reviewers use them; re-customizable cork; nearly indestructible, some 500+ days; new lighter touring "Espresso" liner).
- SNOWBOARDING & SPLITBOARDING: Weston Japow snowboard; Venture Paragon split (1-split quiver); Cardiff Powgoda Pro Carbon split; Jones Ultralight Hovercraft 2.0 split; Spark Surge ST Pro & Arc ST Pro bindings; Jones Mercury FASE & Zenith FASE bindings; Folkrm Tahoma Poles (280 g/pole, 3-piece carbon for splitboarders, extra-long grip).
- APPAREL OUTERWEAR: Trew PRIMO Cosmic & Stella Jackets & Eagle Pants; Flylow Baker Bib (probably most durable outerwear tested; also Chemical Pant); Raide TourTech Shell Jacket & Bib (favorite touring kit for drier climates).
- APPAREL INSULATION: Patagonia Micro Puff & DAS Light Hoodies (PlumaFill warmth-to-weight, more water-resistant than down); Flylow TC Vest (packs to apple size); Patagonia Nano-Air series (active insulation reference; Nano-Air Ultralight Freeride Hoody).
- BASELAYERS: CW-X Stabilyx Joint Support 3/4 Compression Tight (knee support; one reviewer wears daily); Le Bent Featherweight & Heavyweight (merino/bamboo-rayon, good for wool-sensitive); Nuyarn wool baselayers (merino benefits with much better durability).
- GOGGLES: Anon WM1, M2, M3, M4, M5 & Sync; Glade Adapt 3; Julbo Launcher REACTIV; SPY Marauder Elite.
- ACCESSORIES: Scott Team Issue Pole (still unbroken after 10+ years of multiple pairs); GoggleSocs; DB Snow Roller Pro Bag (favorite ski bag 10+ years, hands-free wheeling strap); Skida Brim & Running Hats; Wells Lamont HydraHyde Mittens (all-leather, great value, durable as mitts 2x price); Hestra Leather Fall Line Gloves (hundreds of days; not for very wet climates); BCA Float E2-35 (best all-round avalanche airbag pack tested; supercapacitor Alpride E2, "Side Stash" pockets, radio spot); Patagonia PowSlayer Pack (sleek, hidden ski/helmet carry); Mammut Barryvox S2 beacon (Paul Forward's all-time favorite).

(Index lists "Moonlight Cruiser 120 // 40".)

Closing photos: Sascha Anastas (Snowbird), BLISTER+ member with Luke Koppa (Crested Butte).

## 6. Notable themes / what's new this year
- Structure: 12th edition; NEW "On-Piste Carving Skis" section split out, with "Frontside" redefined as skis between pure carvers and All-Mountain. Best Of awards now reflect reviewer CONSENSUS rather than individual favorites.
- Editorial: Ellsworth's "Two Truths and a Lie" -- gratitude for every snow day + take real climate action; stop casual complaining; use poor conditions to build skills; "pick one real thing" to act on. Continued no-manufacturer-ad policy, "think before you buy," no MSRPs for skis.
- Weight-placement tech: K2's overhauled Mindbender line (96, 101, 111 and women's 96 W/101 W) uses TPU/steel "targeted mass weighting" -- calmer shovels and stability with lower overall weight; Mindbender 96 earns a Best Of. Renoun VibeStop (non-Newtonian) across Endurance/Earhart/Atlas/Citadel. Ferreol's in-house "Scalium" alloy replacing titanal across its line; flax fibers (Surfeur 112).
- Blister's recurring concern: brands making skis lighter; they note mass still correlates with stability, while some brands now build strong, light directional skis; and some brands acknowledging weight matters for chargers (M-Pro 108 Ti "blast from the past," Heritage Lab RC116/R120 ST/AM118/HB122 V2 heavy dampers).
- New/major lines: Blizzard Canvas (100/108/118) -- more playful, light, centered-mount Blizzards with surprising stability; Icelantic Torrent (88/96/102) and women's Tempest (88/94/100) -- far stiffer, more precise than past Icelantics (Torrents replace Pioneers); Armada AntiMatter (100, 100 W, 108, 114) replacing Declivity X; Faction Dancer overhaul with Ti/non-Ti versions (Dancer 89/89 Ti, 99 Ti, 106 Ti); Nordica Unleashed 106 CA replacing 108 and new Dobermann Multipista 80; Volkl Revolt 112; Line Poisson and updated Sakana; Kastle Sublime 93; Heritage Lab Guide 135 and HB122 V2; Folsom S-Class; Fischer "Noize" Curv GT 85; Rossignol Sender 118 (renamed again); J Skis Hotshot overhauled (lighter); RMU Apostle 105 replacing 106; Moment Countach series. Many semi-custom/custom builders featured (Wagner, Folsom, Prior, Parlor, Romp, Shaggy's, FullSend).
- Women's coverage expanded to three sections (More Stable, More Forgiving, Powder), all tested by female reviewers regardless of "women-specific" labeling.
- Boots: shell designs for power transfer (Lange Shadow, Salomon S/Pro Alpha C) gaining favor; BOA proliferation (Blister: not a deciding factor but good for lower-shell tuning); hybrid/cabrio designs (phaenom, Armada AR ONE); flagship boots now down to 22.5; Lange Code 140 sets new AT downhill benchmark; ZipFit liners championed; touring boots trending toward efficient-but-freeride (Scarpa F1 RS, Maestrale RS update); Scarpa F1 LT and 4-Quattro XT W being phased out.
- Bindings: true hybrids (CAST Freetour 2.0, Marker Duke PT, Tyrolia Attack Hybrid, Shift2 13 & new 16) now match alpine downhill; ATK Hy 13 Free a light hybrid-ish standout; ATK Raider still the touring benchmark.
- Skins: industry move to easier-to-rip adhesives (G3 "Channel Bond," Contour Hybrid, BD's revised glue); Pomoca Tour Pro Expedition's dual-zone plush.
- Snowboarding: fast-entry bindings (Jones FASE incl. new Zenith; Nidecker Supermatic); volume-shifted/swallowtail pow boards remain a theme; Jones "Re-Up" recycled-board stringers; Union Direct Connect mounting.
- Outerwear: PFAS-free DWRs (Gore-Tex ePE) bead water for less time industry-wide; durability emphasis (Trew PRIMO, Flylow Baker, Stio Figment).
- Safety: helmet advocacy with Ellsworth's neck-break story; Mips variants (Spherical, 2Vi), WaveCel; multi-norm BC helmets; photochromic and magnetic-swap goggles trickling to cheaper models.
